    "content": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nBecause IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer bridges the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.\nThis scale-dependent interaction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nPer-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations (Level~3 non-local constraints;     "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nBecause IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.\nThis scale-dependent interaction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.\nConstraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp).\nThis is an architectural trade-off of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, first-seen type registration (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.\nBeam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at only 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nThe trade-off represents a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance.\nOntoCoDec provides a runtime guarantee layer---the two approaches are complementary rather than competing.\nLoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\\%, and constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nThe fundamental tension is that per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations, which correspond to Level~3 (non-local) constraints in our taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}).\nGlobal enforcement requires maintaining entity-level type consensus across the full output sequence, exceeding what online per-token masking can achieve.\nBeam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.\n",

[Director 指令] [MF1 最终数据] DFA 边界占位符替换——用实际实体数

Agent 统计结果：
- **SciERC**: median **17** unique entities/doc (P25=12, P75=20, max=36)
- **DocRED**: median **19** unique entities/doc (P25=15, P75=24, max=42)

SciERC 有 6 entity types，DocRED 有 6 entity types。

**请将 §3 Engineering applicability 段的 DFA 边界更新为**：

原来的占位符 "6^10 ≈ 6×10^7" 和 "7^20 > 10^16" 替换为：

- SciERC: 6 types, median 17 entities → **6^17 ≈ 1.6×10^13** DFA states
- DocRED: 6 types, median 19 entities → **6^19 ≈ 6.1×10^14** DFA states

注意：DocRED entity types 是 6 个（PER, ORG, LOC, TIME, NUM, MISC），不是 7 个。如果之前写了 7，改为 6。

```text
13-\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}
14-\label{sec:decidability}
15:We classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.
16:Unlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.
17-OntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
18-
19-\smallskip
20-\noindent
21:Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.
22-We formalize this gap:
23-
24-\smallskip
25-\noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
26:\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
27-
28-\smallskip
--
31-An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
32-A PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint.
33:Intuitively, the entity registry accumulates state during decoding that gives the language a non-regular structure: pumping an arbitrarily long entity sequence would require the automaton to remember each entity's type for subsequent constraint checks, exceeding finite-state capacity (formal pumping argument in Appendix~\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).
34:OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\hfill$\square$
35-
36-\smallskip
37:The proof establishes theoretical impossibility for unbounded entity sets; the following paragraph addresses the finite (practical) case.
38-
39-\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}
40:When the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\mathcal{E}|^{|\text{entities}|})$ states.
41:For SciERC (6 entity types, ${\sim}$10 entities per document), this yields $6^{10} \approx 6{\times}10^7$ states---technically constructable but impractical for online decoding.
42:For DocRED (7 entity types, 20+ entities), $7^{20}$ exceeds $10^{16}$ states, making direct DFA construction intractable.
43:The exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).
44-
45-\noindent\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
--
62-\end{equation}
63-%
64:where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
65:The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
66:The grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.
67-
68-\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
--
76-
77-A JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints ($\mathcal{E}$, $\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.
78:At step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).
79-
80-\subsection{Semantic Layer}
81-\label{sec:semantic}
82-
83:The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
84:\emph{Running example}: for \textsc{Used-for} ($\operatorname{dom}{=}\{\textrm{Method}\}$, $\operatorname{range}{=}\{\textrm{Task}\}$), entity ``ResNet'' (Method) passes the head-type check while Material is blocked; ``image classification'' (Task) passes the tail-type check.
85-
86-\paragraph{Entity registry.}
87:\label{sec:entity-registry}
88:The semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$ adds $(n_i, \tau_i)$ to $\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.
89:The registry uses \emph{first-seen} semantics: when an entity name appears in multiple triples, the type assigned at its first occurrence governs all subsequent constraint checks, making type enforcement sensitive to generation order.
90-At each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
91-\label{sec:position-detection}
--
110-
111-\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}
112:Both layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.
113-
114-\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}

38	
39	\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}
40	When the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\mathcal{E}|^{|\text{entities}|})$ states.
41	For SciERC (6 entity types, ${\sim}$10 entities per document), this yields $6^{10} \approx 6{\times}10^7$ states---technically constructable but impractical for online decoding.
42	For DocRED (7 entity types, 20+ entities), $7^{20}$ exceeds $10^{16}$ states, making direct DFA construction intractable.
43	The exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).
44	
45	\noindent\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
```

        "newStart": 38,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}",
          " When the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states.",
          "-For SciERC (6 entity types, ${\\sim}$10 entities per document), this yields $6^{10} \\approx 6{\\times}10^7$ states---technically constructable but impractical for online decoding.",
          "-For DocRED (7 entity types, 20+ entities), $7^{20}$ exceeds $10^{16}$ states, making direct DFA construction intractable.",
          "+For typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), a DFA would require $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$ states---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.",
          " The exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).",
          " ",
          " \\noindent\\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation})."
        ]
      }
    ],

**问题**：section5_runtime.tex L12 写 "1.18×...1.39× (Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency})"，但 tab:beam-latency（appendix_beam_search.tex）的 baseline 是 greedy **unconstrained**，显示 k=4 = **1.54×**。读者查表会发现矛盾。

主文 1.39× = beam constrained / greedy constrained
附录表 1.54× = beam constrained / greedy unconstrained

**修复**（推荐方案 1）：
- 将主文中引用 tab:beam-latency 的地方改为引用 tab:beam-sweep（如果 beam sweep 表包含 latency 数据），或
- 在主文 latency 引用处加括号说明 baseline："1.39× relative to greedy constrained decoding"，并将 Table 引用改为指向包含正确 baseline 的表
- 如果没有合适的表，就去掉表引用，改为 inline 说明 baseline

SciERC = 6 (Task, Method, Material, Metric, OtherScientificTerm, Generic)
DocRED = 6 (LOC, MISC, NUM, ORG, PER, TIME)

## Warning: 附录 CF count 21 vs 22

appendix_beam_search.tex L136 写 "CF=1.18% (21 cross-field violations)"——21 是 unique entity-doc pairs，主文用 22（total violations）。统一为 22。

8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep
11	With LoRA, beam search~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED at both 8B and 70B scales (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
12	A beam-width sweep ($k \in \{1,2,4,8\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\%, at only 1.18$\times$ greedy latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\times$ latency (Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
13	This yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\%, CF${\leq}$1.4\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\times$, all violations${\to}$0\%).
14	Per-beam memory overhead is $O(k \times |\text{entities}| \times |\text{types}|)$ for independent entity registry copies, negligible at typical document scales.
15	
```

58	\paragraph{Discussion.}
59	For calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.
60	On SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (0.08\%\,$\to$\,0\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\%; Llama: 6.56\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
61	On DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (1.58\%\,$\to$\,0\%), with CF reductions of 74\% and 86\% respectively.
62	Llama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.
63	The CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.
64	
65	Mistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.
66	On SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\%\,$\to$\,13.8\%), though CF drops by 55\% relative (1.27\%\,$\to$\,0.57\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.
67	On DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\% relative (28.4\%\,$\to$\,21.4\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.
68	This contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \cap S(t) = \emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.
69

472-On DocRED, Llama-3.1-8B contributes 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), with entity type errors as the dominant cause (14/33, 42\%).

585-WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} is a document-level event argument extraction benchmark grounded in the DARPA KAIROS ontology.
586:It defines 56 event types, 80 argument roles, and 27 entity types, with 245 (event\_type, role)$\to$allowed\_entity\_types constraints.
587-The grammar layer targets EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and ET; the semantic layer enforces AC (argument constraint) via role-conditioned masking, analogous to RE domain/range enforcement.

    "oldString": "A beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\\%, at only 1.18$\\times$ greedy latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\\times$ latency (Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).",
    "newString": "A beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\\%, at only 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:beam-sweep}; latencies relative to greedy S1).",
    "originalFile": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\n% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep\nWith LoRA, beam search~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED at both 8B and 70B scales (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nA beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\\%, at only 1.18$\\times$ greedy latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\\times$ latency (Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\nThis yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\\%, CF${\\leq}$1.4\\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\\times$, all violations${\\to}$0\\%).\nPer-beam memory overhead is $O(k \\times |\\text{entities}| \\times |\\text{types}|)$ for independent entity registry copies, negligible at typical document scales.\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B-class validation (single-family).}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nThe following results constitute a single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4); cross-family verification (e.g., Qwen-72B) remains future work.\nAll 70B experiments use Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct under AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms constraint enforcement is unaffected by quantization (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\nZero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; R-F1 11--13).\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)\nA four-way ablation reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\\% DR reduction vs.\\ 8B's 24\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), though grammar remains necessary for RV elimination and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained\nLoRA confirms composability: DR\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\\Delta| \\leq 0.20$\\,pp; CF 0.18--1.33\\%); cross-family generalization at 70B scale remains to be verified.\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_docred_200_beam4_s1\nBeam ($k{=}4$) reduces DocRED parse failures (12.5\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.5\\%, R-F1 $+$4.19\\,pp); SciERC controlled comparison isolates pure beam gain at $+$0.28\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence on Qwen3-8B (DR${=}$15.26\\%; other models use the same backend; Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nThe ${\\sim}$15\\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\\% total reduction); trie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\\% parse failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}).\n",

          "+On SciERC (6 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.",

数据审计发现 Table 2 (tab:composability) 的 DocRED 四行全部使用 parse-failure bug 修复前的旧值。SciERC 不受影响。

## Table 2 修正值（16 个 cells）

### Qwen DocRED:
| | DR | CF | R-F1 | E-F1 | ΔDR |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Unconstrained | **0.76** (was 0.56) | **0.12** (was 0.08) | **40.86** (was 40.89) | **82.15** (was 82.35) | --- |
| S1 | **0.01** (was 0.00) | **0.41** (was 0.22) | **40.56** (was 40.79) | **81.43** (was 81.87) | **↓98.7%** (was ↓100%) |

### Llama DocRED:
| | DR | CF | R-F1 | E-F1 | ΔDR |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Unconstrained | 0.49 ✓ | 0.08 (verify) | **43.72** (was 44.55) | **83.93** (was 84.77) | --- |
| S1 | **0.21** (was 0.00) | **0.21** (was 0.26) | **43.78** (was 44.70) | **83.94** (was 84.71) | **↓57%** (was ↓100%) |

## 受影响的正文 claims — 必须同步修改

1. **Abstract L9**: "near-eliminates domain/range violations ($<$0.1%)" → **改为 "DR ≤ 0.21%" 或 "near-zero (<0.25%)"**。0.21% > 0.1%，原 claim 不成立。

2. **§5.1 L88**: "adding S1 yields DR ≤ 0.09%" → **改为 "DR ≤ 0.21%"**

3. **§5.1 composability 段**: "no significant ΔR-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95% CIs ⊆ [-0.69, +0.25] pp" → **CI 范围可能需要更新**（R-F1 变化更大了：Llama DocRED 从 +0.15pp 变为 +0.06pp，但 Qwen DocRED 从 -0.10pp 变为 -0.30pp）。如果无法重算 CI，至少更新点估计。

4. **§5.1 L147**: "36 residual DR violations" → 需核查是否受影响

5. **§1 Contribution 中的任何 DR 范围声明**

6. **§7 Discussion 中引用的 DR 数字**

7. **Appendix tab:bootstrap-effect-sizes**: 如果 CI 是用旧数据算的，标注 "computed from pre-correction values; point estimates updated in Table 2"——DDL 前无法重算。

## 关键注意

**Llama DocRED S1 ΔDR 从 ↓100% 降为 ↓57%**——这是所有 setting 中最差的。但 Qwen DocRED ΔDR 仍为 ↓98.7%，SciERC 两个模型都是 ↓92-100%。所以 claim 需要从 "near-eliminates" 变为 "substantially reduces (57-100%)"，或针对 Llama DocRED 做特殊说明。

在 cross-model validation 的叙事中，Llama DocRED S1 的 0.21% DR 和 ↓57% 是一个 outlier——需要诚实呈现并讨论原因（可能与 DocRED 高 κ schema + Llama entity typing weakness 有关）。

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.
3	These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).
4	
5	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
6	A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
7	
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning near-eliminates domain/range violations ($<$\,0.1\%) at F1 41--47\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\leq$1.4\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.
10	Beam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\times$.
11	In zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\%) but decrease with model calibration.
12	Zero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.
13	\end{abstract}
14

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Lbx83qqrkwxAHzwtpL2rT3`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\% independently.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
20	LoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 41--47\% ($\pm$0.8\,pp, $+$10\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\times$ latency).
21	DR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\sim$31\% (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).
22	
23	OntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
27	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.
28	    \item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR elimination ($\leq$0.09\%) with R-F1 within $\pm$0.8\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
29	    \item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\ 24\% at 8B (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\%, 1.18$\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\times$.
30	\end{enumerate}
31

      "content": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR elimination ($\\leq$0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n    \\item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
      "numLines": 31,

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SquNr7XnUWLe4PSZe8tvma`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.
4	Because IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer bridges the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.
5	This scale-dependent interaction (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\times$ compression; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}).
6	
7	\paragraph{DR$\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}
8	Greedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\,pp)---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement, where first-seen type registration (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent requirements.
9	Beam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\times$ (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
10	The trade-off is a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable, while CF is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
11	
12	\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}
13	GoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance; OntoCoDec provides a complementary runtime guarantee layer.
14	LoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\%, constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.
15	
16	\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\ global enforcement.}
17	Per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations (Level~3 non-local constraints; \S\ref{sec:decidability}).
18	Global enforcement requires entity-level type consensus across the full output, exceeding online per-token masking; beam search approximates this, TCR provides post-hoc correction, and integrating entity-level consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.
19	
```

</details>

phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} establishes that DR violations are pervasive (15--18\\% across datasets) and that constraint layers interact non-additively: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%---the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}.\nOn DocRED (96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), grammar-only \\emph{increases} DR via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing OOV$\\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nCross-model validation (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}) confirms DR drops of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with R-F1 improving in 5/6 settings; post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates.\nDR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nZero-shot evaluation thus establishes violation prevalence and constraint interaction; fine-tuning demonstrates practical elimination.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning (expanded)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nUnder fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\\Delta$DR$<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--69\\% reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nFallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\\% for Llama, confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\\% of zero-shot CF; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  CF quantification (MF1 response)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248, 167 docs), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22, 20 docs)\nUnder zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\\% involve \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\\%) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{Material} (12.3\\%).\nA source breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade-analysis}) shows 58.1\\% originate from hallucinated entities (a model capacity issue), while true cascade errors---where first-seen registration locks an incorrect type---account for only 7.3\\%; correct locks are 2.5$\\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.\nFine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\\times$ latency (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & 0.01 & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$98.7\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.00 & 0.26 & 44.70 & 84.71 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1 (\\%). No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels (Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}); error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\\%) was blocked by constraints, with dominant sources (relation confusion, entity type polysemy) schema-valid and beyond constraint reach (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nAcross fine-tuned settings, 36 residual DR violations decompose into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and relation/direction errors (28\\%); the irreducible floor is $\\sim$3 violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning near-eliminates domain/range violations ($<$\\,0.1\\%) at F1 41--47\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "5845fa8a-d64f-4b68-81ad-5694a19d746b",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "dfd0dd50-ae6f-49be-a090-5337bdd25739",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "ac78f6b2-7117-4a9e-ae54-c62c98d5d158",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex",
    "oldString": "demonstrating DR elimination ($\\leq$0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp",
    "newString": "demonstrating DR reduction to $\\leq$0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.3\\,pp",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 41--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR elimination ($\\leq$0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n    \\item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

      {

        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 25,
        "newLines": 6,

Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.",

Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.",

    "oldString": "LoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency)",
    "newString": "LoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 41--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency)",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR elimination ($\\leq$0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n    \\item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 17,
        "newLines": 7,

given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.\nOur R-F1 range (41--47\\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\\%~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting};    - DFA state bounds: 6^17 ≈ 1.6×10^13 (SciERC), 6^19 ≈ 6.1×10^14 (DocRED) — both datasets have 6 entity types

   - Parse-failure bug fix invalidated all DocRED Table 2 data — Llama DocRED S1 DR went from 0% to 0.21% (ΔDR from ↓100% to ↓57%)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited appendix/references/limitations

       Fine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).

schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",
        "numLines": 19,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 19
      }

{

  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex",
        "content": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 41--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR reduction to $\\leq$0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.3\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n    \\item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 31,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 31
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex"
  },

      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\\leq$\\,0.21\\%) at F1 41--46\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 14,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 14
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex"

        "content": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08  & 15.45\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08  & 14.92\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nGCD-equiv.$^\\dagger$\n  & 15.26 & $\\downarrow$1\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.04  & 15.30\n  & 8.75  & 30.91 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71  & 14.36\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nS1\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90  & \\textbf{10.83}\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nS2\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{9.00}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained; Tot.\\ = DR + CF; bold = best (CF excluded).\nF1 (\\%). S1/S2 = single/two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nGrammar = GCD ceiling~\\cite{geng2023gcd}.\n$\\dagger$vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).\nDeterministic (greedy, $T{=}$0); robustness evidence in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model} and Appendices~\\ref{app:bootstrap-effect-sizes},~\\ref{app:prompt-sensitivity}.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Zero-shot diagnostic (compressed)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} establishes that DR violations are pervasive (15--18\\% across datasets) and that constraint layers interact non-additively: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%---the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}.\nOn DocRED (96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), grammar-only \\emph{increases} DR via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing OOV$\\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nCross-model validation (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}) confirms DR drops of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with R-F1 improving in 5/6 settings; post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates.\nDR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nZero-shot evaluation thus establishes violation prevalence and constraint interaction; fine-tuning demonstrates practical elimination.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning (expanded)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nUnder fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\\Delta$DR$<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--69\\% reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nFallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\\% for Llama, confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\\% of zero-shot CF; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  CF quantification (MF1 response)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248, 167 docs), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22, 20 docs)\nUnder zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\\% involve \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\\%) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{Material} (12.3\\%).\nA source breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade-analysis}) shows 58.1\\% originate from hallucinated entities (a model capacity issue), while true cascade errors---where first-seen registration locks an incorrect type---account for only 7.3\\%; correct locks are 2.5$\\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.\nFine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\\times$ latency (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & 0.01 & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$98.7\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1 (\\%). No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels (Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}); error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\\%) was blocked by constraints, with dominant sources (relation confusion, entity type polysemy) schema-valid and beyond constraint reach (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nAcross fine-tuned settings, 36 residual DR violations decompose into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and relation/direction errors (28\\%); the irreducible floor is $\\sim$3 violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions.       "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks.
